=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer n representing the dimensions of an n x n grid, with the origin at the bottom-left corner of the grid. You are also given a 2D array of coordinates rectangles, where rectangles[i] is in the form [start_x, start_y, end_x, end_y], representing a rectangle on the grid. Each rectangle is defined as follows:

(start_x, start_y): The bottom-left corner of the rectangle.
(end_x, end_y): The top-right corner of the rectangle.

Note that the rectangles do not overlap. Your task is to determine if it is possible to make either two horizontal or two vertical cuts on the grid such that:

Each of the three resulting sections formed by the cuts contains at least one rectangle.
Every rectangle belongs to exactly one section.

Return true if such cuts can be made; otherwise, return false.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 5, rectangles = [[1,0,5,2],[0,2,2,4],[3,2,5,3],[0,4,4,5]]
Output: true
Explanation:

The grid is shown in the diagram. We can make horizontal cuts at y = 2 and y = 4. Hence, output is true.

Example 2:

Input: n = 4, rectangles = [[0,0,1,1],[2,0,3,4],[0,2,2,3],[3,0,4,3]]
Output: true
Explanation:

We can make vertical cuts at x = 2 and x = 3. Hence, output is true.

Example 3:

Input: n = 4, rectangles = [[0,2,2,4],[1,0,3,2],[2,2,3,4],[3,0,4,2],[3,2,4,4]]
Output: false
Explanation:
We cannot make two horizontal or two vertical cuts that satisfy the conditions. Hence, output is false.

 
Constraints:

3 <= n <= 10^9
3 <= rectangles.length <= 10^5
0 <= rectangles[i][0] < rectangles[i][2] <= n
0 <= rectangles[i][1] < rectangles[i][3] <= n
No two rectangles overlap.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

But let's not rush. Need ensure "rectangles do not overlap" but projections can overlap. Merging intervals along one axis by overlap works because if intervals overlap along axis, rectangles cannot be separated by a cut perpendicular to that axis. Need prove.

Let's formalize for vertical cuts: Consider x-intervals [x1,x2]. If two intervals overlap (x1_i < x2_j and x1_j < x2_i), then any vertical cut coordinate c that separates them into different sections would need to be either between them. More rigorous: Suppose rectangle A assigned to left of cut c1 and rectangle B assigned to right of cut c2 (c1<c2). Then A's x2 <= c1 and B's x1 >= c2. Since c1<c2, A.x2 < B.x1, so intervals disjoint with A entirely before B. If A and B overlap, impossible. Similarly if B left and A right. If one in middle and one left/right, also need separation. Thus any two rectangles with overlapping x-intervals must be in same section. This relation transitive; merge connected components of overlap graph. A cut can only be placed between components. If there are at least 3 components, we can choose cuts between them. If fewer, cannot have 3 sections because each component must be entirely in one section and sections nonempty; if number of components <3, cannot assign to 3 nonempty sections? Let's think of proof for final explanation maybe not needed in code, but final answer only code. But maybe need include code only. The final answer should be code block. We can include no explanation. But before final, let's ensure code syntax.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(intervals):
            intervals.sort()
            groups = 0
            curr_end = -1
            for l, r in intervals:
                if groups == 0:
                    groups = 1
                    curr_end = r
                elif l < curr_end:
                    curr_end = max(curr_end, r)
                else:
                    groups += 1
                    curr_end = r
                if groups >= 3:
                    return True
            return False

        vertical = [[x1, x2] for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]
        horizontal = [[y1, y2] for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]
        return can_cut(vertical) or can_cut(horizontal)
```
Need note intervals.sort() mutates list. Fine. Could avoid modifying? no issue.

Potential issue with groups initialization: If groups==0 and l maybe -? no. If first interval l<curr_end? curr_end=-1, no. Good.

Could simplify:
```
def can_cut(intervals):
    intervals.sort()
    merged_end = intervals[0][1]
    cnt = 1
    for l,r in intervals[1:]:
        if l < merged_end:
            merged_end = max(merged_end, r)
        else:
            cnt += 1
            merged_end = r
        if cnt >=3: return True
    return False
```
Need if intervals length >=3? rectangles length >=3, so okay. But if intervals empty? no. Use robust.

We argued overlapping intervals cannot be separated by any cut, but let's formalize with two cuts. Suppose intervals I and J overlap. Could I be in left section and J in middle? For I left, need I.r <= c1. For J middle, need J.l >= c1 and J.r <= c2. Since I.r <= c1 <= J.l. If I and J overlap, I.r > J.l (because overlap condition I.l < J.r and J.l < I.r; specifically I.r > J.l). Contradiction. I left, J right: I.r <= c1 < c2 <= J.l, so I.r < J.l, no overlap. I middle, J right: I.r <= c2 <= J.l, no overlap. I right, J left: J.r <= c1 < c2 <= I.l, no overlap. Thus any two intervals in different sections must be disjoint with one entirely before the other. Therefore overlapping intervals must be same section. This relation is transitive: if I overlaps J and J overlaps K, I,J,K same section. So connected components of overlap graph must each be contained in one section. Since there are three nonempty sections, need at least three components? Let's maybe search memory: LeetCode 3394 "Check if Grid can be Cut into Sections" official solution:
```
class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def check(axis):
            arr = []
            for rect in rectangles:
                arr.append((rect[axis], rect[axis+2]))
            arr.sort()
            cnt = 0
            end = -1
            for l,r in arr:
                if l >= end:
                    cnt += 1
                end = max(end, r)
            return cnt >= 3
        return check(0) or check(1)
```

- l2>=2 cnt2 end3
- l2>=3 false end4
cnt2. Good.

```
def can_cut(intervals):
    intervals.sort()
    cnt = 0
    curr_end = -1
    for l, r in intervals:
        if l >= curr_end:
            cnt += 1
        curr_end = max(curr_end, r)
        if cnt >= 3:
            return True
    return False
```

Let's ensure no syntax errors:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(intervals: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
            intervals.sort()
            components = 0
            current_end = -1
            for start, end in intervals:
                if start >= current_end:
                    components += 1
                current_end = max(current_end, end)
                if components >= 3:
                    return True
            return False

        vertical = [[x1, x2] for x1, _, x2, _ in rectangles]
        horizontal = [[y1, y2] for _, y1, _, y2 in rectangles]
        return can_cut(vertical) or can_cut(horizontal)
```
Need note using `_` multiple times in list comprehension is okay? In Python, `_` is a regular variable, can use multiple times. But in a list comprehension, `for x1, _, x2, _ in rectangles` is allowed? Yes, repeated `_` is allowed; it binds same name twice? Actually in tuple unpacking, you can use same name multiple times; the second assignment overwrites, but no issue. However, some linters? Fine. To avoid confusion, use `for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles` and build both in one pass? Could do:
```
vertical = []
horizontal = []
for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles:
    vertical.append([x1, x2])
    horizontal.append([y1, y2])
```
This is clear. Or use comprehensions with explicit variables:
```
vertical = [[rect[0], rect[2]] for rect in rectangles]
horizontal = [[rect[1], rect[3]] for rect in rectangles]
```
This avoids underscore. Let's use explicit for clarity.

```
def check(rectangles, dim):
    arr = []
    for r in rectangles:
        arr.append([r[dim], r[dim+2]])
    arr.sort()
    cnt = 0
    max_end = -1
    for s,e in arr:
        if max_end <= s:
            cnt += 1
        max_end = max(max_end, e)
    return cnt >= 3
```

Now, let's think if there is any issue with using `current_end` as max end of all intervals processed, not reset, in the simple count. Let's test a case where a later interval starts before current_end but current_end is from a previous component that ended after current component start? Wait if current_end is max of all processed intervals, and we haven't reset, could it include an end from a previous component that is greater than the start of the current component? But if a previous component's end is greater than current component's start, then current component's start < previous end, so they would overlap, meaning they should be same component. The algorithm would not have started a new component at current component because start >= current_end would be false. So if a new component was started, its start >= current_end at that time, which was max end of all previous intervals. Thus no previous component extends beyond current start. After starting new component, current_end becomes r > start >= old current_end, so max remains current component's end. Thus no issue. Good.

Let's maybe think if there is a need to check both orientations in one function with axis index:
```
def check(axis):
    intervals = [(r[axis], r[axis+2]) for r in rectangles]
    intervals.sort()
    cnt=0
    cur=-1
    for s,e in intervals:
       if s >= cur:
          cnt += 1
       cur = max(cur,e)
       if cnt >=3: return True
    return False
return check(0) or check(1)
```
This is concise. Use axis 0 for x (vertical cuts), axis 1 for y (horizontal cuts). Since rectangle tuple [start_x, start_y, end_x, end_y], axis+2 gives end coordinate. Good.

Need maybe if `axis=0`, intervals = [(x1,x2)], vertical cuts. If `axis=1`, intervals = [(y1,y2)], horizontal cuts. Good.

Let's decide final code style. I'd write:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def possible(axis: int) -> bool:
            intervals = []
            for rect in rectangles:
                intervals.append((rect[axis], rect[axis + 2]))
            intervals.sort()
            
            groups = 0
            current_end = -1
            for start, end in intervals:
                if start >= current_end:
                    groups += 1
                current_end = max(current_end, end)
                if groups >= 3:
                    return True
            return False
        
        return possible(0) or possible(1)
```
This uses tuples; sorting tuples fine. It doesn't need n. Good.

This is clear. Use helper `possible(intervals)` not axis. Let's decide final code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(intervals: List[tuple]) -> bool:
            intervals.sort()
            groups = 0
            current_end = -1
            for start, end in intervals:
                if start >= current_end:
                    groups += 1
                current_end = max(current_end, end)
                if groups >= 3:
                    return True
            return False

        x_intervals = [(x1, x2) for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]
        y_intervals = [(y1, y2) for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]
        return can_cut(x_intervals) or can_cut(y_intervals)
```
Type hint `List[tuple]` maybe okay but `tuple` without type args. Could omit helper type hint to avoid import? We already import List. Use `def can_cut(intervals):` simpler. But final code with type hints okay.

Potential issue: `List[tuple]` is not a valid generic? `List[tuple]` is valid (tuple as type). But no need. Use no helper type hint.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(intervals: List[tuple]) -> bool:
            intervals.sort()
            groups = 0
            current_end = -1

            for start, end in intervals:
                if start >= current_end:
                    groups += 1
                current_end = max(current_end, end)

                if groups >= 3:
                    return True

            return False

        vertical = [(x1, x2) for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]
        horizontal = [(y1, y2) for x1, y1, x2, y2 in rectangles]

        return can_cut(vertical) or can_cut(horizontal)
```
